Ruijsenaars wavefunctions as modular group matrix coefficients
Authors: Philippe Di Francesco, Rinat Kedem, Sergey Khoroshkin, Gus Schrader, Alexander Shapiro
Published: 2024

ABSTRACT
We give a description of the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunctions of the 2-particle hyperbolic Ruijsenaars system as matrix coefficients for the order 4 element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\in SL(2,{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}S∈SL(2,Z) acting on the Hilbert space of GL(2) quantum Teichmüller theory on the punctured torus. The GL(2) Macdonald polynomials are then obtained as special values of the analytic continuation of these matrix coefficients. The main tool used in the proof is the cluster structure on the moduli space of framed GL(2)-local systems on the punctured torus, and an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z)-equivariant embedding of the GL(2) spherical DAHA into the quantized coordinate ring of the corresponding cluster Poisson variety.

Introduction

In this article, we propose a description of two integrable systems, the 2-particle quantum hyperbolic Ruijsenaars system and the 2-particle q-difference open Toda chain, in terms of the quantum Teichmüller theory of the punctured torus. The mapping class group of the latter is isomorphic to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z), and understanding its action on the corresponding quantum mechanical Hilbert space enables us to reduce questions about the Ruijsenaars system into simpler ones about the Toda system. In particular, in Theorem 5.5 we obtain a description of the (suitably renormalized, cf. Proposition 5.2) Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunctions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau (\varvec{\lambda })$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ) of the hyperbolic Ruijsenaars Hamiltonians as the matrix coefficient of the element\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S=\begin{pmatrix}0& -1\\ 1& 0\end{pmatrix}\in SL(2,{\mathbb {Z}}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S=0-110∈SL(2,Z)between two states known as q-Whittaker functions:1.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau (\varvec{\lambda }) = \left\langle \Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}, S\Psi _{\varvec{\mu }}\right\rangle . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ)=Ψλ,SΨμ.The q-Whittaker functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}$$\end{document}Ψλ are familiar objects in the well-developed algebraic and analytic theory of the open q-difference Toda chain, see, e.g., the following non-exhaustive list of references [8, 11, 14–17, 23, 27, 28]. In particular, as \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\lambda }}$$\end{document}λ ranges over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {R}}^2/S_2$$\end{document}R2/S2 they furnish a complete, orthogonal set of eigendistributions for the Toda chain Hamiltonians.

Let us now discuss these integrable systems in a little more detail and outline our strategy for realizing them via quantum Teichmüller theory. The N-particle relativistic hyperbolic Ruijsenaars system [26] is a system of N commuting difference operators acting on meromorphic functions of N complex variables. The analytic theory of this integrable system was studied extensively in [19–21] and [1–4], where the joint eigenfunction transform was constructed and shown to define a unitary equivalence between appropriate Hilbert spaces.

The hyperbolic Ruijsenaars operators are close relatives of the Macdonald difference operators from the theory of symmetric functions. The latter operators preserve the space of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_N$$\end{document}SN-symmetric polynomial functions, where they act diagonalizably with distinct eigenvalues, and their joint eigenfunctions are the well-known Macdonald polynomials.

As discovered by Cherednik in [6], it is useful to regard the Macdonald difference operators as generators of a commutative subalgebra in a 2-parameteric family of non-commutative algebras \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{q,t}(GL_N)$$\end{document}Hq,t(GLN) known as the double affine Hecke algebra (DAHA) associated with the group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GL_N$$\end{document}GLN. The DAHA is a quotient of the braid group of N points on a punctured 2-torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^2{\setminus } D^2$$\end{document}T2\D2, which is responsible for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z)-symmetry of Macdonald theory.

In this article, we focus on the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=2$$\end{document}N=2, in which the connection to the geometry of the torus becomes even easier to describe: the fiber at \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q=1$$\end{document}q=1 of the spherical subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}$$\end{document}SHq,t of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GL_2$$\end{document}GL2 DAHA recovers the coordinate ring of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GL_2({\mathbb {C}})$$\end{document}GL2(C)-character variety for the punctured torus, with the parameter t related to the eigenvalues of the monodromy around the puncture. In this realization of the algebra, the Macdonald operators are quantizations of the observables given by the fundamental traces of the monodromy of a GL(2)-local system around the (0, 1)-curve on the torus. The commuting subalgebra in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}$$\end{document}SHq,t associated in the same way to the (1, 0)-curve is identified with that generated by the operators of multiplication by the elementary symmetric functions acting on the ring of 2-variable symmetric polynomials.

To reconnect this algebraic picture with the analytic Ruijsenaars theory, we recall from the work of Fock and Goncharov [13] that the punctured torus character variety (or more precisely, a decorated version thereof, where the local systems are equipped with extra data of a Borel reduction at the puncture) is a cluster Poisson variety. Hence, its quantization delivers a (projective) unitary Hilbert space representation of the torus mapping class group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z), which acts by explicit quantum cluster transformations. The (0, 1)-curve generators of the spherical DAHA \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}$$\end{document}SHq,t, which correspond to the Macdonald–Ruijsenaars operators in Cherednik’s representation, act by unbounded, symmetric operators on this Hilbert space. On the other hand, we show the (1, 0)-curve generators act in the cluster representation by the quantum open Toda chain Hamiltonians, which leads directly to formula (1.1). The matrix coefficient realization of the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunctions makes immediate most of their important properties, including the “bispectral” and “Poincaré duality” symmetries \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\lambda }}^{\tau }({\varvec{\mu }})=\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Φλτ(μ)=Φμτ(λ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^{-\tau }({\varvec{\lambda }})=\Phi _{\varvec{\lambda }}^\tau ({\varvec{\mu }})$$\end{document}Φμ-τ(λ)=Φλτ(μ), see Corollaries 5.3 and 5.6.

From the physical point of view, the formula (1.1) expresses the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunctions as scalar products between eigenstates of two quantum mechanical integrable systems given by traces of holonomies along the (1, 0) and (0, 1)-curves on the torus, respectively. In other words, the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunction is the transition matrix between two geometrically natural bases in the Hilbert space for quantum Teichmüller theory on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^2{\setminus } D^2$$\end{document}T2\D2. This transition matrix realizes the action of the S-move generator in the corresponding projective representation of the Moore–Seiberg groupoid and has been previously computed by Teschner and Vartanov, see formula (6.30) of [33].

In this sense, formula (1.1) is quite analogous to the well-known description of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SU_2$$\end{document}SU2 Racah–Wigner q-6j symbols which arises in an almost identical way, except that the punctured torus is replaced by the 4-punctured sphere. The semiclassical asymptotics of such scalar products were described geometrically in [25], where the two classical integrable systems determine two presentations of the character variety as Lagrangian fibrations, and the asymptotic expansion of the scalar product takes the form of a sum over intersection points of the two corresponding Lagrangian fibers.

In Sect. 6, we obtain q-Whittaker and Macdonald polynomials, respectively, as special values of analytically continued q-Whittaker and Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunctions. In a similar fashion, we also recover the Harish–Chandra series solutions for the 2-particle quantum open Toda chain and the 2-particle quantum hyperbolic Ruijsenaars system. We conclude this article with a discussion of the N-particle case, which we shall return to in a separate publication.

Ruijsenaars and open Toda integrable systems

In this section, we recall the definition of the two integrable systems that play a central role in the paper: the two-particle hyperbolic Ruijsenaars system and the open q-difference Toda chain.

Ruijsenaars system

\usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \mapsto \Lambda ^{-1}$$\end{document}Λ↦Λ-1. Their eigenbasis in the latter space is given by the celebrated symmetric Macdonald polynomials, which can be recovered as the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars wavefunctions restricted to a lattice, see Section 6 for more details.

Remark 2.1

Consider the Macdonald–Ruijsenaars measure\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_g({\varvec{\lambda }}) = S_2(i(\lambda _1-\lambda _2)) S_2(i(\lambda _2-\lambda _1)+g), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}mg(λ)=S2(i(λ1-λ2))S2(i(λ2-λ1)+g),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S_2$$\end{document}S2 is the Barnes double sine function from equation (A.9). The corresponding bilinear pairing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \cdot ,\cdot \right) _R$$\end{document}·,·R and Hermitian pairing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot ,\cdot \right\rangle _R$$\end{document}·,·R are defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( f,g\right) _R = \int _{\mathbb {R}^2}f({\varvec{\lambda }})g(-{\varvec{\lambda }})m_g({\varvec{\lambda }}) d{\varvec{\lambda }}, \qquad \left\langle f,g\right\rangle _R = \int _{\mathbb {R}^2}f({\varvec{\lambda }})\overline{g({\varvec{\lambda }})}m_g({\varvec{\lambda }}) d{\varvec{\lambda }}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f,gR=∫R2f(λ)g(-λ)mg(λ)dλ,f,gR=∫R2f(λ)g(λ)¯mg(λ)dλ.Fig. 1Regions of unitarity of the Ruijsenaars system in the g-plane

Regions of unitarity of the Ruijsenaars system in the g-plane

As shown in, e.g., [2], for any periods \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _1, \omega _2$$\end{document}ω1,ω2, and the coupling constant g in the strip of analyticity of the Ruijsenaars system, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< \operatorname {Re}(g) < \omega _1+\omega _2$$\end{document}0<Re(g)<ω1+ω2, the Macdonald operators are essentially self-adjoint with respect to the bilinear pairing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \cdot ,\cdot \right) _R$$\end{document}·,·R. Moreover, as shown in [3], when the coupling constant g is real, the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars functions are orthogonal and complete with respect to the pairing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot ,\cdot \right\rangle _R$$\end{document}·,·R. It is immediate from (A.1) and (A.6) that for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in i\mathbb {R}$$\end{document}τ∈iR the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars functions satisfy\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau (-{\varvec{\lambda }}) = \zeta _{inv} e^{4\pi i \tau ^2} \overline{\textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pμτ(-λ)=ζinve4πiτ2Pμτ(λ)¯,where the constant \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\zeta _{inv}$$\end{document}ζinv is given by (A.2), and hence,\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( \textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ,\textrm{P}_{\varvec{\nu }}^\tau \right) _R = \zeta _{inv} e^{4\pi i \tau ^2} \left\langle \textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ,\textrm{P}_{\varvec{\nu }}^\tau \right\rangle _R. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pμτ,PντR=ζinve4πiτ2Pμτ,PντR.Thus, we have two regimes of unitarity of the Ruijsenaars system, and Macdonald operators are symmetric in both of them. In the first regime, the coupling constant g is real and satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$0< g < -ic_b$$\end{document}0<g<-icb, equivalently \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ is imaginary with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left| \tau \right| < c_b/2$$\end{document}τ<cb/2, and the scalar product is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot ,\cdot \right\rangle _R$$\end{document}·,·R. In the second regime \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g = i(\tau -c_b)$$\end{document}g=i(τ-cb) with an arbitrary real \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ, and the scalar product is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \cdot ,\cdot \right\rangle _{Sk}$$\end{document}·,·Sk, see (2.3). In [19–21] and [1–4], the Ruijsenaars system was studied in the first regime. In the present text, we focus on the second. In Fig. 1, we visualize the two regimes for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _1=b$$\end{document}ω1=b, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega _2=b^{-1}$$\end{document}ω2=b-1, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega =-ic_b$$\end{document}ω=-icb.

Notation 2.3

Throughout the paper, we will often consider contour integrals of the form\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \int _{C} \prod _{j,k}\frac{\varphi (t-a_j)}{\varphi (t-b_k)}f(t)dt, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∫C∏j,kφ(t-aj)φ(t-bk)f(t)dt,where f(t) is some entire function. Unless otherwise specified, the contour C in such an integral is always chosen to be passing below the poles of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (t-a_j)$$\end{document}φ(t-aj) for all j, above the poles of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (t-b_k)^{-1}$$\end{document}φ(t-bk)-1 for all k, and escaping to infinity in such a way that the integrand is rapidly decaying.

Spherical double affine Hecke algebra

Recall that the elliptic braid group is the fundamental group of the configuration space of N points on the torus \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T^2$$\end{document}T2. The double affine Hecke algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{q,t}$$\end{document}Hq,t for GL(N) is the quotient of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Q}}(q,t)$$\end{document}Q(q,t)-group algebra of the elliptic braid group by the Hecke relations\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (T_i - t)(T_i + t^{-1}) = 0, \quad i=1,\ldots N, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(Ti-t)(Ti+t-1)=0,i=1,…N,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_i$$\end{document}Ti are the standard “local” generators corresponding to those of the planar braid group. In addition to these local generators, there are also “global” ones, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X_i$$\end{document}Xi and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_i$$\end{document}Yi, corresponding to the loops in the configuration space transporting the i-th marked point once along, respectively, the (1, 0)- and the (0, 1)-curves on the torus. Being a quotient of the elliptic braid group, the algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{q,t}$$\end{document}Hq,t carries an action of the modular group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2;Z) by algebra automorphisms.

We skip the precise definition of the spherical subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}$$\end{document}SHq,t of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{q,t}$$\end{document}Hq,t and refer the reader to [6] for details. Instead, following loc. cit. we recall that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}{q,t}$$\end{document}SHq,t admits a faithful representation into the algebra of symmetric q-difference operators in variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Lambda _1,\Lambda _2)$$\end{document}(Λ1,Λ2). The spherical subalgebra contains elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{(\pm k,0)}, E_{(0,\pm k)}$$\end{document}E(±k,0),E(0,±k) corresponding to spherical versions of the k-th power sum symmetric functions in the standard generators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$X^{\pm }_i,Y^{\pm }_i$$\end{document}Xi±,Yi± of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{q,t}$$\end{document}Hq,t, respectively. As shown in [31], the algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}$$\end{document}SHq,t is generated over \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {Q}}(q,t)$$\end{document}Q(q,t) by elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{(\pm 1,0)}, E_{(0,\pm 1)}$$\end{document}E(±1,0),E(0,±1), see [5, Corollary 6.1].

Under Cherednik’s representation, these generators are mapped to the following q-difference operators:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E_{(1,0)}&\longmapsto e_1({\varvec{\lambda }}),&E_{(-1,0)}&\longmapsto e_1({\varvec{\lambda }})e_2({\varvec{\lambda }})^{-1}, \\ E_{(0,1)}&\longmapsto M^{\tau }_1,&E_{(0,-1)}&\longmapsto M^{\tau }_1\left( M^{\tau }_2\right) ^{-1}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E(1,0)⟼e1(λ),E(-1,0)⟼e1(λ)e2(λ)-1,E(0,1)⟼M1τ,E(0,-1)⟼M1τM2τ-1.The action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2;Z) preserves the spherical subalgebra: For each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v\in {\mathbb {Z}}^2$$\end{document}v∈Z2, there is a corresponding element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_v\in {\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}$$\end{document}Ev∈SHq,t, and we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\cdot E_v = E_{gv}$$\end{document}g·Ev=Egv for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g\in SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}}),~v\in {\mathbb {Z}}^2$$\end{document}g∈SL(2;Z),v∈Z2.

Strategy

Let us now outline in more detail the logic of the remainder of the paper. The double affine Hecke algebra formalism suggests that the problem of diagonalizing the Macdonald difference operators can be solved by constructing a unitary representation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho $$\end{document}ρ of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2;Z) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2_{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {R}^n, m({\varvec{\lambda }})d{\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Lsym2(Rn,m(λ)dλ) compatible with its action on the spherical DAHA. Indeed, the action of the element S would then intertwine the Macdonald operators with the operators of multiplication by elementary symmetric functions:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M^{\tau }_j \circ \rho (S) = \rho (S) \circ e_j({\varvec{\lambda }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mjτ∘ρ(S)=ρ(S)∘ej(λ).The eigenbasis for the latter is given by delta-distributions. Here, we must note that while delta-distributions are clearly not in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2_{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {R}^n, m({\varvec{\lambda }})d{\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Lsym2(Rn,m(λ)dλ), it is only expected that the eigenfunctions of an unbounded operator lie in the space of tempered distributions rather than in the Hilbert space itself. Thus, the eigenbasis for the Macdonald operator would consist of the images of delta-distributions under the S transformation:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M^{\tau }_j \rho (S) \delta ({\varvec{\lambda }}-{\varvec{\mu }}) = e_j({\varvec{\mu }}) \cdot \rho (S) \delta ({\varvec{\lambda }}-{\varvec{\mu }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mjτρ(S)δ(λ-μ)=ej(μ)·ρ(S)δ(λ-μ).Our strategy for constructing the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2;Z) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2_{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {R}^n, m({\varvec{\lambda }})d{\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Lsym2(Rn,m(λ)dλ) is to identify the latter with the unitary equivalent representation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^n, d\varvec{x})$$\end{document}L2(Rn,dx) via the Whittaker transform \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {W}$$\end{document}W. As we will show, the action of generators of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}$$\end{document}SHq,t on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^n, d{\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}L2(Rn,dx) coincides with that of certain elements of the quantized coordinate ring of a cluster Poisson variety. The representation of the latter comes equipped with a compatible action of the group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2;Z), which acts on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^n, d{\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}L2(Rn,dx) by explicit unitary quantum cluster transformations. Thus, the action \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho (S)$$\end{document}ρ(S) on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2_{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {R}^n, m({\varvec{\lambda }})d{\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Lsym2(Rn,m(λ)dλ) can be defined as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho (S) = \mathcal {W}I_S \mathcal {W}^*, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ(S)=WISW∗,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_S$$\end{document}IS denotes the action of S on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^n, d{\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}L2(Rn,dx), see (2.16). Expressing the eigenfunctions of Macdonald operators as\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m({\varvec{\lambda }})^{-1} \rho (S) \delta ({\varvec{\lambda }}-{\varvec{\mu }}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m(λ)-1ρ(S)δ(λ-μ),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m(\lambda )^{-1}$$\end{document}m(λ)-1 is the normalization constant, and using the fact that\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {W}^* \delta ({\varvec{\lambda }}-{\varvec{\mu }}) = \Psi _{\varvec{\mu }}({\varvec{x}}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W∗δ(λ-μ)=Ψμ(x),we see that the matrix coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\langle \Psi _\lambda , I_S \Psi _\mu \right\rangle $$\end{document}Ψλ,ISΨμ give an eigenbasis for the Macdonald operators.2.16After reviewing the general construction of quantum cluster varieties in Sect. 3, the quantum cluster algebra needed to describe the spherical DAHA will be obtained from the moduli space of framed GL(2)-local systems on the punctured torus in Sect. 4. In Sect. 5, we use the matrix coefficient presentation to rederive the formula for the Ruijsenaars wavefunctions along with some of its properties. Finally, in Sect. 6 we recover Macdonald polynomials as special values of analytically continued Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars wavefunctions evaluated on an integer lattice. In a similar way, we recover q-Whittaker polynomials from q-Whittaker functions, as well as the corresponding Harish–Chandra series.

Quantum cluster varieties

In this section, we review the definition of quantum cluster varieties and their representations. For more details on these subjects, we refer the reader to the foundational paper [13]. We only need skew-symmetric quantum cluster algebras with integer-valued forms, which we incorporate in the definition of a seed.

Definition 3.1

A seed is a datum \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} I is a finite set;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\cdot ,\cdot )$$\end{document}(·,·) is a skew-symmetric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {Z}$$\end{document}Z-valued form on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ;

\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left\{ e_i \,|\, i \in I\right\} $$\end{document}ei|i∈I is a basis for the lattice \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda $$\end{document}Λ.

Definition 3.2

We say that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Theta ,\Theta ')$$\end{document}(Θ,Θ′) are equivalent if the isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{e'} {\varvec{e}}^{-1} :\Lambda \simeq \Lambda '$$\end{document}e′e-1:Λ≃Λ′ is an isometry, that is, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(e_i,e_j)_{\Lambda } = (e'_i, e'_j)_{\Lambda '}$$\end{document}(ei,ej)Λ=(ei′,ej′)Λ′ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i,j \in I$$\end{document}i,j∈I. We define a quiver to be an equivalence class of seeds.

The vertices corresponding to the subset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_0$$\end{document}I0 are called frozen and are drawn as squares, while those corresponding to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I {\setminus } I_0$$\end{document}I\I0 are referred to as mutable and are drawn as circles.

If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ,\Theta '$$\end{document}Θ,Θ′ are two seeds with non-degenerate skew forms representing the same quiver, then the isometry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Lambda \rightarrow \Lambda '$$\end{document}Λ→Λ′, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_i\mapsto e_i'$$\end{document}ei↦ei′ determines a canonical isomorphism of Heisenberg algebras \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota :\mathfrak {heis}_\Lambda \rightarrow \mathfrak {heis}_{\Lambda '}$$\end{document}ι:heisΛ→heisΛ′. So by the irreducibility of the canonical representations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}},{\mathcal {H}}'$$\end{document}H,H′, there is a unique projective unitary equivalence \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {I}} :\mathcal {H}\rightarrow \mathcal {H}'$$\end{document}I:H→H′ such that3.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho '(\iota (a))\circ {\mathbb {I}}= {\mathbb {I}} \circ \rho (a) \qquad {\text {for all}}\qquad a\in \mathfrak {heis}_\Lambda , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ′(ι(a))∘I=I∘ρ(a)foralla∈heisΛ,where both sides are understood as maps between the corresponding classical Schwartz spaces in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}_\Theta ,{\mathcal {H}}_{\Theta '}$$\end{document}HΘ,HΘ′. This shows that the data \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Lambda ,\mathfrak {heis}_\Lambda ,\mathcal {H})$$\end{document}(Λ,heisΛ,H) associated with a non-degenerate quiver is unique up to unique isomorphism. Because of this, we will often abuse language and speak of “the” Heisenberg algebra, or Hilbert space, associated with a given quiver.

When the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \cdot ,\cdot \right) $$\end{document}·,· has nonzero kernel Z, the Heisenberg algebra has a family of irreducible \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$*$$\end{document}∗-representations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}_{\chi }$$\end{document}Hχ parametrized by central characters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi :Z \rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}χ:Z→R. In this case, we need to enrich the data of a quiver in order to unambiguously speak of its Heisenberg algebra and representation as explained above. Indeed, unless the central characters are compatible, the isomorphism in (3.2) cannot exist.

Definition 3.3

A seed with central character is a pair \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Theta ,\chi )$$\end{document}(Θ,χ) where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta $$\end{document}Θ is a seed and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\chi :Z\rightarrow \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}χ:Z→R is a linear functional on the kernel Z of the corresponding skew-form. We say that two seeds \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Theta ,\chi )$$\end{document}(Θ,χ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\Theta ',\chi ')$$\end{document}(Θ′,χ′) with central character are equivalent if the underlying seeds are equivalent, and the canonical isometry \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota :\Lambda \rightarrow \Lambda '$$\end{document}ι:Λ→Λ′ of lattices intertwines the central characters: \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( \chi '\circ \iota \right) |_Z = \chi $$\end{document}χ′∘ι|Z=χ. A quiver with central character is an equivalence class of seeds with central character.

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Theta ,\Theta '$$\end{document}Θ,Θ′ be seeds representing quivers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q,Q'$$\end{document}Q,Q′ (possibly with central characters). We say that the quiver \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q'$$\end{document}Q′ is the mutation of Q in direction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$k\in I{\setminus } I_0$$\end{document}k∈I\I0 if the map3.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mu _k :\Lambda \longrightarrow \Lambda ', \qquad e_i \longmapsto {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} -e'_k & {\text {if}} \; i=k, \\ e'_i + \max \{(e_i,e_k),0\}e'_k & {\text {if}} \; i \ne k \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}μk:Λ⟶Λ′,ei⟼-ek′ifi=k,ei′+max{(ei,ek),0}ek′ifi≠kis an isometry intertwining the central characters. It is easy to see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q'=\mu _k(Q)$$\end{document}Q′=μk(Q) if and only if \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ Q=\mu _k(Q')$$\end{document}Q=μk(Q′). The mutation class of a quiver Q, which we denote by the bold symbol \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{Q}}$$\end{document}Q, is the set of all quivers related to Q by some finite sequence of mutations.

To each quiver mutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _k$$\end{document}μk, we associate an isomorphism of quantum tori\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath}
