\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _q(Y)$$\end{document}Ψq(Y) is the (compact) quantum dilogarithm function (A.17). The fact that conjugation by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _q\left( Y'_{k}\right) $$\end{document}ΨqYk′ yields a birational automorphism is guaranteed by the integrality of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\cdot , \cdot )$$\end{document}(·,·) and the functional equations (A.18).

Definition 3.4

An element of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}^q_Q$$\end{document}TQq is said to be universally Laurent if its image under any finite sequence of quantum cluster mutations is contained in the corresponding quantum torus algebra. The universally Laurent algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {L}^q_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}LQq is the algebra of universally Laurent elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}^q_Q$$\end{document}TQq.

If q is a complex number, we can also consider the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {C}}$$\end{document}C-algebra given by the corresponding specialization of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {L}^q_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}LQq. We abuse notation and denote this algebra by the same symbol.

Definition 3.5

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b\in {\mathbb {R}}_{>0}$$\end{document}b∈R>0 and consider the specializations\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hbar = b, \quad q = e^{\pi ib^2},\quad {{\tilde{q}}} = e^{\pi ib^{-2}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ħ=b,q=eπib2,q~=eπib-2,so that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$q,{{\tilde{q}}}$$\end{document}q,q~ are complex numbers on the unit circle. The algebra\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {L}_{{\varvec{Q}}} = \mathbb {L}^{q, {{\tilde{q}}}}_{{\varvec{Q}}} = \mathbb {L}^q_{{\varvec{Q}}} \otimes _{{\mathbb {C}}} \mathbb {L}^{{{\tilde{q}}}}_{{\varvec{Q}}} \end{aligned}$$\end{document}LQ=LQq,q~=LQq⊗CLQq~is called the modular double of the universally Laurent algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {L}^q_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}LQq. The maximal joint domain \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}_Q\subset {\mathcal {H}}_Q$$\end{document}SQ⊂HQ for the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {L}_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}LQ is called the Fock–Goncharov Schwartz space.

Remark 3.6

In a more general setup, when the bilinear form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\cdot ,\cdot )$$\end{document}(·,·) is not required to be integer-valued, the cross-relations between elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}^q_Q$$\end{document}TQq and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}^{{{\tilde{q}}}}_Q$$\end{document}TQq~ are set to be\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y_j {{\widetilde{Y}}}_k = e^{2 \pi i \varepsilon _{kj}} {{\widetilde{Y}}}_k Y_j. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}YjY~k=e2πiεkjY~kYj.

The collection of quantum charts \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}^q_Q$$\end{document}TQq with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q \in {\varvec{Q}}$$\end{document}Q∈Q, together with quantum cluster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X-mutations is often referred to as the quantum cluster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X-variety. We regard the quantum charts as the quantized algebras of functions on the toric charts in the atlas for the classical cluster Poisson variety. The quantum charts form an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell $$\end{document}ℓ-regular tree with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell = |I {\setminus } I_0|$$\end{document}ℓ=|I\I0|, and the cluster mutations quantize the gluing data between adjacent charts. The universally Laurent algebra is the quantum analog of the algebra of global functions on the cluster variety. Unless otherwise specified in what follows, we will simply write “cluster variety” for quantum cluster \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {X}$$\end{document}X-variety—the same applies to variables, charts, mutations, etc.

The (quasi-)cluster modular groupoid associated with a cluster variety is defined as follows.

Definition 3.7

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q,Q'$$\end{document}Q,Q′ be two quivers with the same label sets \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(I,I_0)$$\end{document}(I,I0). We define a generalized permutation to be a monomial isomorphism of quantum tori \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta :\mathcal {T}_{Q}\rightarrow \mathcal {T}_{Q'}$$\end{document}ϑ:TQ→TQ′ such that for some permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma :(I{\setminus } I_0)\rightarrow (I{\setminus } I_0)$$\end{document}σ:(I\I0)→(I\I0) of the non-frozen directions we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta (Y_i) = Y'_{\sigma (i)}$$\end{document}ϑ(Yi)=Yσ(i)′ for all \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i\in I{\setminus } I_0$$\end{document}i∈I\I0. If the quivers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q,Q'$$\end{document}Q,Q′ are equipped with central characters, we require these to be intertwined by the monomial map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ.

In particular, the non-frozen submatrices of the matrices \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon $$\end{document}ε, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varepsilon '$$\end{document}ε′ for quivers Q, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q'$$\end{document}Q′ related by a generalized permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ are conjugate under an actual permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma $$\end{document}σ of the non-frozen set \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I{\setminus } I_0$$\end{document}I\I0.

By the same construction as (3.2) with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ playing the role of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota $$\end{document}ι, we associate with each generalized permutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ a projective unitary transformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\mathbb {I}}_\vartheta :{\mathcal {H}}_Q\longrightarrow {\mathcal {H}}_{Q'}. $$\end{document}Iϑ:HQ⟶HQ′.

Definition 3.8

Let \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q,Q'$$\end{document}Q,Q′ be two quivers with corresponding quantum tori \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}_Q,\mathcal {T}_{Q'}$$\end{document}TQ,TQ′ as in (3.1). A quasi-cluster transformation with source Q and target \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q'$$\end{document}Q′ is a non-commutative birational isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {T}_Q\dashrightarrow \mathcal {T}_{Q'}$$\end{document}TQ⤏TQ′ which can be factored as a composition of cluster mutations and generalized permutations.

Definition 3.9

The quasi-cluster modular groupoid is the groupoid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_{\varvec{Q}}$$\end{document}GQ whose objects are quivers \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q \in {\varvec{Q}}$$\end{document}Q∈Q (possibly with central characters), and whose morphisms are quasi-cluster transformations. The quasi-cluster modular group, denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Gamma _{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}ΓQ, is the automorphism group of an object in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}GQ.

Remark 3.10

Any element of the quasi-cluster modular group restricts to an automorphism of the universally Laurent algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {L}_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}LQ.

Denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textbf{Hilb}$$\end{document}Hilb the category whose objects are Hilbert spaces and whose morphisms are projective unitary transformations. At this point, we have associated with each object Q in the groupoid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}GQ a Hilbert space \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}_Q$$\end{document}HQ, and associated unitary intertwiners \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {I}}_\vartheta $$\end{document}Iϑ to those morphisms in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}GQ given by generalized permutations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vartheta $$\end{document}ϑ. In [13], it was further shown how to associate intertwiners \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {I}}_{\mu _k}$$\end{document}Iμk to the mutation morphisms in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}GQ, which send \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}_Q$$\end{document}SQ to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}_{Q'}$$\end{document}SQ′ and give rise to well-defined functor \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {I}} :\mathcal {G}_{{\varvec{Q}}}\rightarrow \textbf{Hilb}$$\end{document}I:GQ→Hilb, that is, a projective representation of the groupoid \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {G}_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}GQ.

Let us briefly recall the construction of the intertwiner corresponding to a mutation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _k :Q\rightarrow Q'$$\end{document}μk:Q→Q′. Once again, the construction (3.2) with the monomial isomorphism \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mu _k'$$\end{document}μk′ playing the role of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota $$\end{document}ι provides a projective unitary transformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ {\mathbb {I}}_{\mu _k'} :{\mathcal {H}}_Q\rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}_{Q'}. $$\end{document}Iμk′:HQ→HQ′. On the other hand, by the property (A.1) of the quantum dilogarithm function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (z)$$\end{document}φ(z) it follows that any self-adjoint operator y on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {H}}$$\end{document}H defines a unitary operator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (y) :{\mathcal {H}}\simeq {\mathcal {H}}$$\end{document}φ(y):H≃H. The unitary transformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb {I}}_{\mu _k} :{\mathcal {H}}_Q \rightarrow {\mathcal {H}}_{Q'}$$\end{document}Iμk:HQ→HQ′ is then defined to be3.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\mathbb {I}}_{\mu _k} = {\mathbb {I}}_{\mu _k'}\circ \varphi (-y_k)^{-1}=\varphi (y'_k)^{-1}\circ {\mathbb {I}}_{\mu _k'}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Iμk=Iμk′∘φ(-yk)-1=φ(yk′)-1∘Iμk′.That this recipe is indeed well defined is a difficult theorem, which is the main analytical result of the paper [13]. In the following section, we will illustrate how to use it in practice with a concrete example.

Quantum Teichmüller theory for the punctured torus

In this section, we illustrate the previous definitions in our main example, the moduli space of framed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GL_2$$\end{document}GL2 local systems on a punctured torus.

Quantized moduli space of framed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GL_2$$\end{document}GL2-local systems on a punctured torus

Consider the quiver Q shown in Fig. 2 and the associated cluster variety. The kernel of the corresponding skew-form is spanned by the vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$z = -e_1-e_2-e_3$$\end{document}z=-e1-e2-e3. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau \in {\mathbb {R}}$$\end{document}τ∈R, we write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Q_\tau $$\end{document}Qτ for the quiver with central character defined by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {X}}_\tau (z)={2}\tau $$\end{document}Xτ(z)=2τ. The latter corresponds to a relation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y_1Y_2Y_3 = q^2e^{-4\pi b \tau }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Y1Y2Y3=q2e-4πbτ.Fig. 2Quiver Q

Quiver Q

The mutable part of the quiver is known as the Markov quiver and is isomorphic to any of its mutations. The Markov quiver describes the cluster structure on the moduli space of framed \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL_2$$\end{document}SL2-local systems on a punctured torus, where the framing data consist of a flag (a line in rank 1), invariant under the monodromy around the puncture, see [12]. The additional frozen vertices will be used to parametrize the determinants of holonomies around loops on the punctured torus, as we now explain.

At the quantum level, the algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {L}_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}LQ contains elements corresponding to the traces and determinants of holonomies around closed simple curves. For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(m,n) \in \mathbb {Z}^2$$\end{document}(m,n)∈Z2, we denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{(m,n)}$$\end{document}L(m,n) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _{(m,n)}$$\end{document}Δ(m,n) the elements corresponding, respectively, to the trace and the determinant of the monodromy along the (m, n)-curve on the punctured torus. These elements can be constructed explicitly as follows. We choose a basis in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_1(T^2{\setminus } D^2;\mathbb {Z})\simeq {\mathbb {Z}}^2$$\end{document}H1(T2\D2;Z)≃Z2 so that the horizontal cycle, passing from left to right through nodes 1 and 2 in Fig. 3, is of homology class (1, 0). Then, we set4.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{(1,0)} = Y_{e_4} + Y_{e_2+e_4} + Y_{e_1+e_2+e_4}, \qquad \Delta _{(1,0)} = Y_{e_1+e_2+2e_4}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(1,0)=Ye4+Ye2+e4+Ye1+e2+e4,Δ(1,0)=Ye1+e2+2e4.As shown in [29, Proposition 3.4], elements \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{(1,0)}$$\end{document}L(1,0) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _{(1,0)}$$\end{document}Δ(1,0) are universally Laurent:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} L_{(1,0)}, ~\Delta _{(1,0)} \in \mathbb {L}_{{\varvec{Q}}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}L(1,0),Δ(1,0)∈LQ.Fig. 3Cluster quiver from a punctured torus

Cluster quiver from a punctured torus

Formulas (4.1) can be written down with the help of the following standard combinatorial recipe, see, e.g., [12, Section 9]. Consider the bipartite graph on a cylinder, shown in the left of Fig. 4. On the right, we show a dual quiver, which we use to define a cluster variety, with edges directed in such a way that the white vertex of the bipartite graph stays on the right as we traverse an edge. The direction of edges of the bipartite graph is additional data, which allows one to express a monodromy matrix M in cluster coordinates. Namely, we set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M_{ij} = \sum _{p: \, i \rightarrow j} Y_{\operatorname {wt}(p)}, \qquad {\text {where}}\qquad \operatorname {wt}(p) = \sum _{f \, {\text {below}} \, p} e_f. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mij=∑p:i→jYwt(p),wherewt(p)=∑fbelowpef.The first sum in the formula above is taken over all paths in the directed bipartite graph from i-th source to the j-th sink, while the second is taken over all faces lying below the path \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {wt}(p)$$\end{document}wt(p). Note that the matrix M only depends on variables 1, 2, and 4, but not on the variable 0. Since the monodromy matrix is only defined up to conjugation, we shall be looking at the symmetric functions of its eigenvalues: the trace and the determinant in our case, which are equal to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{1,0}$$\end{document}L1,0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _{1,0}$$\end{document}Δ1,0, respectively. Let us also note that in order to work with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL_2$$\end{document}SL2 rather than \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GL_2$$\end{document}GL2 local systems one only needs to specialize \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$y_4 = -\frac{1}{2}(y_1+y_2)$$\end{document}y4=-12(y1+y2).Fig. 4Directed network and dual cluster quiver

Directed network and dual cluster quiver

Remark 4.1

Comparing formulas (2.8) and (4.8), we see that the Q-system evolution operator is identified with the action \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_{\sigma _+}$$\end{document}Iσ+ of the (1, 0)-cycle Dehn twist, justifying our notation for the former.

Proposition 4.2

The element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_S^2$$\end{document}IS2 acts on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^2,d{\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}L2(R2,dx) by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (I_S^2f)({\varvec{x}}) = f({\varvec{x}}^*) \qquad {\text {where}}\qquad {\varvec{x}}^* = (-x_2,-x_1). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(IS2f)(x)=f(x∗)wherex∗=(-x2,-x1).In particular, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_S$$\end{document}IS is a unitary automorphism of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L^2(\mathbb {R}^2,d{\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}L2(R2,dx) of order 4.

Proof

The proof consists of a direct verification based on the definitions (4.10) and (4.12), the inversion formula (A.6), the factorization\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {F}= i e^{-\pi i (p_1^2+p_2^2)} e^{-\pi i (x_1^2+x_2^2)} e^{-\pi i (p_1^2+p_2^2)}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F=ie-πi(p12+p22)e-πi(x12+x22)e-πi(p12+p22),and the relation\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (e^{2\pi i p_1p_2}e^{2\pi i x_1x_2}e^{2\pi i p_1p_2}f)({\varvec{x}}) = (\mathcal {F}f)({\varvec{x}}^*). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(e2πip1p2e2πix1x2e2πip1p2f)(x)=(Ff)(x∗).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Cluster realization of spherical double affine Hecke algebra

We now algebraically interpret the computations from the previous section as defining an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2;Z)-equivariant injective homomorphism from the spherical subalgebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}$$\end{document}SHq,t of the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {gl}}_2$$\end{document}gl2 double affine Hecke algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {H}_{q,t}$$\end{document}Hq,t into the universally Laurent algebra \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {L}^q_{{\varvec{Q}}}$$\end{document}LQq. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {gl}}_n$$\end{document}gln version of this homomorphism will be given in a forthcoming publication.

Theorem 4.3

There is an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z)-equivariant injective homomorphism\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \iota :{\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t} \hookrightarrow \mathbb {L}^q_{{\varvec{Q}}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ι:SHq,t↪LQq,defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \iota \left( E_{(\pm 1,0)}\right) = L_{(\pm 1,0)} \qquad {\text {and}}\qquad \iota \left( E_{(0,\pm 1)}\right) = L_{(0,\pm 1)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ιE(±1,0)=L(±1,0)andιE(0,±1)=L(0,±1).

Proof

Comparing formulas (2.9) and (2.15) with the ones (4.7) derived in the previous section shows that for each \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(a,b)\in \{(\pm 1,0),(0,\pm 1)\}$$\end{document}(a,b)∈{(±1,0),(0,±1)}, the inverse Whittaker transform\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {W}^* :L^2_{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {R}^2, m({\varvec{\lambda }})d{\varvec{\lambda }}) \longrightarrow L^2(\mathbb {R}^2, d{\varvec{x}}) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W∗:Lsym2(R2,m(λ)dλ)⟶L2(R2,dx)intertwines the action of the spherical DAHA generator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{(a,b)}$$\end{document}E(a,b) in the Cherednik representation with that of the quantum cluster algebra element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{(a,b)}$$\end{document}L(a,b) in the representation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathcal {S}}_\tau \subset {\mathcal {H}}_\tau $$\end{document}Sτ⊂Hτ of the quantum cluster variety. Hence, Theorem 2.4, together with the faithfulness of the two representations at hand, guarantees that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\iota $$\end{document}ι is an injective homomorphism. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z)-equivariance is manifest, since for any \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g \in SL(2,{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}g∈SL(2,Z) and any primitive vector \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$v \in \mathbb {Z}^{ 2}$$\end{document}v∈Z2 we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L_{gv} = gL_v$$\end{document}Lgv=gLv and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_{gv} = gE_v$$\end{document}Egv=gEv. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We therefore see that in the context of quantum Teichmüller theory for the punctured torus as formulated in Sect. 4, the Whittaker functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}({\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}Ψλ(x) are interpreted as eigenfunctions for the quantized traces of monodromy around the (1, 0)-curve. Equation (4.5) is interpreted as the intertwining relation4.13\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M^{\tau }_j \mathcal {W}I_S = \mathcal {W}I_S H_j. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}MjτWIS=WISHj.between the Toda and Macdonald operators under the unitary equivalence\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {W}\circ I_S :L^2(\mathbb {R}^2, d{\varvec{x}}) \longrightarrow L^2_{\textrm{sym}}(\mathbb {R}^2, m({\varvec{\lambda }})d{\varvec{\lambda }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W∘IS:L2(R2,dx)⟶Lsym2(R2,m(λ)dλ).In particular, the distributions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_S \Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}({\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}ISΨλ(x) provide a complete set of eigenfunctions for the quantized traces of monodromy around the (0, 1)-curve.

Ruijsenaars wavefunctions as matrix coefficients

In this section, we show how the eigenfunctions of Macdonald operators, the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars functions, can be derived from those of Toda Hamiltonians using the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2;Z) on the Hilbert space assigned by quantum Teichmüller theory to the punctured torus. More specifically, we present a formula for the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunctions as matrix coefficients between Toda eigenfunctions for the element of S of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z) considered in the previous section. This provides a functional version of Cherednik’s \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z) action in the DAHA theory, see [6], where S acts as the Macdonald–Fourier integral transform with kernel being Macdonald polynomial, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma _+$$\end{document}σ+ acts via multiplication by the Gaussian \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma $$\end{document}γ. We remark that the details on the use of the Whittaker–Fourier transform for the q-Whittaker case can be found in [10].

Recall from the previous section the eigenfunctions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_S\cdot \Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}({\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}IS·Ψλ(x) for the quantized traces of monodromy around the (0, 1)-curve. We first compute these eigenfunctions more explicitly.

In view of Proposition 4.2, the second formula for the action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_S^2$$\end{document}IS2 follows from comparing the Gauss–Givental presentation (2.11) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{{\varvec{\lambda }}^*}({\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}Ψλ∗(x) with the Mellin–Barnes presentation (2.12) for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}({\varvec{x}}^*)$$\end{document}Ψλ(x∗). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

We denote by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ) the following matrix coefficient5.1\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }}) = \left\langle \Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}, I_S \Psi _{\varvec{\mu }}\right\rangle \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ)=Ψλ,ISΨμof the element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S \in SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}S∈SL(2,Z). The following proposition gives an explicit calculation of this matrix element. An equivalent formula has been obtained by Teschner and Vartanov in Section 6.5.4 of [33].

Proposition 5.2

The matrix coefficient \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ) is related to the Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Pμτ(λ) via the formula5.2\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }}) = \zeta \zeta _s^{-1} e^{2\pi i\tau ^2} \Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pμτ(λ)=ζζs-1e2πiτ2Φμτ(λ).In particular, the integral defining the matrix coefficient (5.1) is convergent.

Corollary 5.3

The Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars eigenfunction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Pμτ(λ) is symmetric in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\lambda }}$$\end{document}λ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\mu }}$$\end{document}μ, and satisfies\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }}) = \textrm{P}_{\varvec{\lambda }}^{-\tau }({\varvec{\mu }}) \qquad {\text {and}}\qquad \overline{\textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})} = \zeta _{inv}^{-1}e^{-4\pi i \tau ^2}{\textrm{P}_{\varvec{\mu }}^{-\tau }({\varvec{\lambda }}^*)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pμτ(λ)=Pλ-τ(μ)andPμτ(λ)¯=ζinv-1e-4πiτ2Pμ-τ(λ∗).

Remark 5.4

The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ) is symmetric in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\lambda }}$$\end{document}λ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\mu }}$$\end{document}μ, and satisfies 5.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})} = \Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^{-\tau }({\varvec{\lambda }}^*). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ)¯=Φμ-τ(λ∗).

The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ) is symmetric in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\lambda }}$$\end{document}λ, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\mu }}$$\end{document}μ, and satisfies 5.3\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \overline{\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})} = \Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^{-\tau }({\varvec{\lambda }}^*). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ)¯=Φμ-τ(λ∗).

The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ) satisfies the duality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }}) = \Phi _{\varvec{\lambda }}^{-\tau }({\varvec{\mu }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ)=Φλ-τ(μ).

The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ) satisfies the duality \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }}) = \Phi _{\varvec{\lambda }}^{-\tau }({\varvec{\mu }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ)=Φλ-τ(μ).

Proof

By Point 3 of Theorem 5.5, the symmetry in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\lambda }}$$\end{document}λ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\mu }}$$\end{document}μ is inherited from that of the Whittaker functions, and the equality (5.3) is easily checked using the unitarity property (A.6) of the non-compact quantum dilogarithm. For Point 2, we use the symmetries in Point 1, together with the unitarity of the S-transformation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I_S$$\end{document}IS and the second part of Proposition 5.1:\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Phi _{\varvec{\mu }}^\tau ({\varvec{\lambda }})= & \langle \Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }},I_S \Psi _{\varvec{\mu }} \rangle \\= & \langle I_S\Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}, I_S^2\Psi _{\varvec{\mu }} \rangle = \langle I_S\Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}, \Psi _{{\varvec{\mu }}^*} \rangle = \overline{ \langle \Psi _{{\varvec{\mu }}^*}, I_S\Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }} \rangle } = \overline{ \Phi _{\varvec{\lambda }}^\tau ({\varvec{\mu }}^*)} = \Phi _{\varvec{\lambda }}^{-\tau }({\varvec{\mu }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φμτ(λ)=⟨Ψλ,ISΨμ⟩=⟨ISΨλ,IS2Ψμ⟩=⟨ISΨλ,Ψμ∗⟩=⟨Ψμ∗,ISΨλ⟩¯=Φλτ(μ∗)¯=Φλ-τ(μ).\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Macdonald polynomials and Harish–Chandra series

We finish the paper by showing how the GL(2) q-Whittaker and Macdonald polynomials can be obtained, respectively, as special values of the analytic continuations of Whittaker and Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars functions. We also show that by closing the contour of integration and calculating residues of these functions, we recover the Harish–Chandra series in both Whittaker and Macdonald case.

Whittaker polynomials from Whittaker functions

As observed in [28], the Whittaker function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Psi _{\varvec{\lambda }}({\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}Ψλ(x) becomes entire after multiplying by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (x_2-x_1)$$\end{document}φ(x2-x1). Definition 6.1

The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {gl}}_2$$\end{document}gl2 q-Whittaker polynomial \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W_{{\varvec{n}}}({\varvec{z}};q)$$\end{document}Wn(z;q) is defined by6.4\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W_{{\varvec{n}}}({\varvec{z}}; q) = \sum _{k=0}^{n_2-n_1}\left( {\begin{array}{c}n_2-n_1\\ k\end{array}}\right) _q z_1^{n_1+k}z_2^{n_2-k}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Wn(z;q)=∑k=0n2-n1n2-n1kqz1n1+kz2n2-k,with the q-binomial coefficient given by (A.16).

Theorem 6.2

The value of the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\widetilde{\Psi }}}_{\varvec{\lambda }}({\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}Ψ~λ(x) at the point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{x}} = (c^+_{n_1,{{\tilde{n}}}_1},c^-_{n_2,{{\tilde{n}}}_2})$$\end{document}x=(cn1,n~1+,cn2,n~2-) is given by6.5\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\widetilde{\Psi }}}_{\varvec{\lambda }}(c^+_{n_1,{{\tilde{n}}}_1},c^-_{n_2,{{\tilde{n}}}_2}) = W_{{\varvec{n}}}(e^{2\pi b \lambda _1},e^{2\pi b \lambda _2};q^{-2}) W_{\varvec{\tilde{n}}}(e^{2\pi b^{-1} \lambda _1},e^{2\pi b^{-1} \lambda _2};{{\tilde{q}}}^{-2}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ψ~λ(cn1,n~1+,cn2,n~2-)=Wn(e2πbλ1,e2πbλ2;q-2)Wn~(e2πb-1λ1,e2πb-1λ2;q~-2),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\tilde{q}}} = e^{\pi i b^{-2}}$$\end{document}q~=eπib-2.

Proof

Let us first consider the value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\widetilde{\Psi }}}_{\varvec{\lambda }}({\varvec{x}})$$\end{document}Ψ~λ(x) at the point\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {\varvec{x}} = (c^+_{n_1,{{\tilde{n}}}_1},c^-_{n_2,{{\tilde{n}}}_2}-\epsilon ) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}x=(cn1,n~1+,cn2,n~2--ϵ)for some \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \in \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}ϵ∈R. Finally, using (A.4) and (A.7) to calculate the sum of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(n+1)({{\tilde{n}}}+1)$$\end{document}(n+1)(n~+1) residues, we arrive at the equality (6.5). \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\square $$\end{document}□

Macdonald polynomials from Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars functions

The derivation of Macdonald polynomials goes along the similar lines as that of Whittaker polynomials.

Definition 6.3

Given a partition \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{n}}$$\end{document}n as in (6.2), the symmetric \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathfrak {gl}}_2$$\end{document}gl2 Macdonald polynomial is defined by\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_{{\varvec{n}}}({\varvec{x}}; t,q) = \sum _{r=0}^{n_2-n_1} \frac{\left( q^{n_2-n_1};q^{-1}\right) _r \left( t;q\right) _r}{\left( q^{n_2-n_1-1}t;q^{-1}\right) _r \left( q;q\right) _r} x_1^{n_1+r} x_2^{n_2-r}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pn(x;t,q)=∑r=0n2-n1qn2-n1;q-1rt;qrqn2-n1-1t;q-1rq;qrx1n1+rx2n2-r,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(X;q)_n$$\end{document}(X;q)n denotes the q-Pochhammer symbol, see (A.15).

Consider the renormalized Hallnäs–Ruijsenaars function6.6\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\widetilde{\Phi }}}^\tau _{\varvec{\mu }}({\varvec{\lambda }}) = \zeta e^{-4\pi i \mu \tau _-} \frac{\varphi (-2\mu -c_b)}{\varphi (-2\mu -2\tau )} \textrm{P}^\tau _{\varvec{\mu }}({\varvec{\lambda }}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Φ~μτ(λ)=ζe-4πiμτ-φ(-2μ-cb)φ(-2μ-2τ)Pμτ(λ).

Theorem 6.4

Given a pair of partitions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{n}$$\end{document}n, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varvec{{{\tilde{n}}}}$$\end{document}n~ as in (6.2), the value of the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${{\widetilde{\Phi }}}^\tau _{\varvec{\mu }}({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}Φ~μτ(λ) at the point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\mu }} = (-\tau -c^+_{n_1,{{\tilde{n}}}_1},\tau -c^-_{n_2,{{\tilde{n}}}_2})$$\end{document}μ=(-τ-cn1,n~1+,τ-cn2,n~2-) is given by the product6.7\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P_{{\varvec{n}}}(e^{2\pi b\lambda _1}, e^{2\pi b\lambda _2}; e^{2\pi b(2\tau +c_b)}, e^{2\pi i b^2}) P_{\varvec{{{\tilde{n}}}}}(e^{2\pi b^{-1}\lambda _1}, e^{2\pi b^{-1}\lambda _2}; e^{2\pi b^{-1}(2\tau +c_b)}, e^{2\pi i b^{-2}}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pn(e2πbλ1,e2πbλ2;e2πb(2τ+cb),e2πib2)Pn~(e2πb-1λ1,e2πb-1λ2;e2πb-1(2τ+cb),e2πib-2),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$c^{\pm }_{r,s}$$\end{document}cr,s± is defined by (6.3).

Proof

We show the conclusion of the theorem holds for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _-\ne 0$$\end{document}τ-≠0, with the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau _-=0$$\end{document}τ-=0 case then following from the analyticity of both sides in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tau $$\end{document}τ.
