Remark 6.5

One can now see that the series, obtained from (2.5) by computing residues at all poles of the integrand of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$ \pm \mu -\tau + c_{r,s}^-$$\end{document}±μ-τ+cr,s-, is proportional (up to a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\mu }}$$\end{document}μ) to the series\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} e^{-2 \pi i {\varvec{\lambda }} \cdot {\varvec{\mu }}} e^{2 \pi i \tau _+(\lambda _1-\lambda _2)} P_{q,t}\left( e^{2 \pi b (\lambda _2-\lambda _1)}\Big \vert e^{2 \pi b (\mu _1-\mu _2)}\right) P_{{{\tilde{q}}},{{\tilde{t}}}}\left( e^{2 \pi b^{-1} (\lambda _2-\lambda _1)}\Big \vert e^{2 \pi b^{-1} (\mu _1-\mu _2)}\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e-2πiλ·μe2πiτ+(λ1-λ2)Pq,te2πb(λ2-λ1)|e2πb(μ1-μ2)Pq~,t~e2πb-1(λ2-λ1)|e2πb-1(μ1-μ2),which has a nonzero radius of convergence when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b \notin \mathbb {R}$$\end{document}b∉R. More precisely, the radius is equal to\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} R = {\left\{ \begin{array}{ll} \min \left( \left| t^2/q^2\right| , \left| {{\tilde{q}}}^2/{{\tilde{t}}}^2\right| \right) , & {\text {if}} \; |q|<1, \\ \min \left( \left| q^2/t^2\right| , \left| {{\tilde{t}}}^2/{{\tilde{q}}}^2\right| \right) , & {\text {if}} \; |q|>1, \end{array}\right. } \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R=mint2/q2,q~2/t~2,if|q|<1,minq2/t2,t~2/q~2,if|q|>1,where we set\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} {{\tilde{t}}} = e^{\pi b^{-1} (2\tau +c_b)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}t~=eπb-1(2τ+cb).

Finally, evaluating the expression6.9\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \zeta e^{-4\pi i \mu \tau _-} \frac{\varphi (-2\mu -c_b)}{\varphi (-2\mu -2\tau )} \left( \textrm{P}_+ + \textrm{P}_-\right) \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ζe-4πiμτ-φ(-2μ-cb)φ(-2μ-2τ)P++P-at point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\varvec{\mu }} = (-\tau -c^+_{n_1,\tilde{n}_1},\tau -c^-_{n_2,{{\tilde{n}}}_2})$$\end{document}μ=(-τ-cn1,n~1+,τ-cn2,n~2-), we see that the first summand vanishes, since the dilogarithm \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\varphi (-2\mu -2\tau )$$\end{document}φ(-2μ-2τ) has a pole, while the second summand truncates to the first factor in (6.7).

Conclusion

Let us start by recalling our strategy for deriving the joint eigenfunctions for the Macdonald–Ruijsenaars operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_j^\tau $$\end{document}Mjτ, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$j=1,2$$\end{document}j=1,2. We first observe that these operators are obtained from the action of particular elements of the spherical DAHA \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}(GL_2)$$\end{document}SHq,t(GL2) in a Hilbert space representation of the latter. Using cluster theory, we show that this representation carries an action of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z) by unitary automorphisms, which intertwine the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z) action on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}(GL_2)$$\end{document}SHq,t(GL2) and can be computed explicitly. From this, we deduce that the action of the element \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S\in SL(2;{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}S∈SL(2;Z) intertwines the action of the Macdonald–Ruijsenaars operators with that of the multiplication operators \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e_j({\varvec{\lambda }})$$\end{document}ej(λ) and so delivers the sought-for unitary joint eigenfunction transform for the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M_j^\tau $$\end{document}Mjτ.

Our explicit computation of the action of the modular group element S hinges on the identification of the spherical DAHA \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}(GL_2)$$\end{document}SHq,t(GL2) with the quantized coordinate ring of the moduli space of decorated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GL_2({\mathbb {C}})$$\end{document}GL2(C)-local systems on the punctured torus. Indeed, by the work of Fock and Goncharov, the corresponding classical moduli space carries a cluster structure, on which the mapping class group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,{\mathbb {Z}})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z) acts by cluster transformations.

Generalizing our approach to the N-particle case requires identifying the spherical DAHA \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$${\mathbb{S}\mathbb{H}}_{q,t}(GL_N)$$\end{document}SHq,t(GLN) with the quantized coordinate ring of an appropriate cluster variety. The variety in question parametrizes \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$GL_N(\mathbb {C})$$\end{document}GLN(C)-local systems on the punctured torus, whose monodromy g around the puncture satisfies \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\operatorname {rank}(g-t\operatorname {Id}) \leqslant 1$$\end{document}rank(g-tId)⩽1, equipped with a flat section of the associated \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathbb {P}^{N-1}$$\end{document}PN-1-bundle near the puncture. Although for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N>2$$\end{document}N>2 this constrained moduli space does not fall within the Fock–Goncharov construction, it nonetheless admits a cluster parametrization in which the mapping class group \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$SL(2,\mathbb {Z})$$\end{document}SL(2,Z) again acts by cluster transformations. The combinatorics of this action is much more complicated than in the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=2$$\end{document}N=2 case, however, making it a challenge to write explicit formulas for integral kernels such as the ones used in this paper to describe the action of S.

Despite this difficulty, there are potential advantages offered by the richer combinatorics in higher rank. Indeed, for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N=2$$\end{document}N=2 the mutable parts of all quivers in the cluster atlas on the moduli space coincide with the Markov quiver, while for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N>2$$\end{document}N>2 the corresponding quivers are of infinite mutation type. Each mutation class of the quiver affords a different factorization of the action of S into cluster transformations, and hence a different presentation for the joint eigenfunctions. We hope that this plethora of equivalent representations can prove useful in the study of the eigenfunctions, and hope to return to the issue in a subsequent publication.
